                                                                                                                                                ′
LHS =     ∏        Ev( Gx ) β(Gx ) ·     ∏         Ev( Gy ) β(Gy ) ·     ∏         Ev( Gz ) β(Gz ) · (−1)| pure(σ) β |+| pure(τ ) β |+| pure(σ ) β | ,
        Gx ∈G(σ)                       Gy ∈G(τ )                       Gz ∈G(τ )


Comparing with the RHS, it is easy to observe that the (−1) factor equal on both sides as


                        | pure(σ) β | + | pure(τ ) β | + | pure(σ′ ) β | = | pure(α)| β


since all three sets on the LHS are pairwise disjoint and their union is exactly pure(α) β . It
remains for us to show a bĳection between the collection of rainbow components on the
LHS and the collection on the RHS.

   To show from left to right, this direction is immediate as we notice each rainbow
component in σ, τ, σ′ is also a rainbow component in α = σ ◦ τ ◦ σ′ . For the other
direction, it is crucial to observe that each component in α is in exactly one of σ, τ, σ′ . This

                                                            328
is immediate for rainbow components that do not touch on vertices in Uτ ∪ Vτ as they
are either in G(σ) or G(σ′ ) if they are connected to Uα or Vα , and if neither, it is in G(τ ).
Finally, for any rainbow component Gi that touches on vertices in Uτ ∪ Vτ = S L ∪ SR , each
such component is in G(τ ) only: the rainbow component does not have edges in σ as
the V ( Gi ) ∩ S L is not incident to any rainbow-color edge in σ as otherwise the rainbow
(leftwards) extension can be further applied, violating the assumption that σ is the ﬁnal
left-shape at the end of the factorization process. The proof holds identically for SR and
σ′ .


Structure of Left/Right Shapes

Deﬁnition 8.5.6 (Color-Left/Right-Shape). For a left-shape σ with coloring CP, it is a color-
left-shape if

       1. Vσ is not incident to any cross-color edge in CP;

       2. Vσ is the unique MVS for σ (equiv. σ is a left-shape) .

Analogously, for a right-shape σ with coloring CP, it is a color-right-shape if

       1. σ is a right shape;

       2. Uσ is not incident to any cross-color edge in CP.

       Similar to how we abstract out labels in [n] from ribbons to shapes, the above process
can be abstracted out to color partition by identifying the collection of vertices that have the
same color and ignoring the speciﬁc label in [k ]. Therefore, for the following combinatorial
charging analysis, we will work in the level of color-partition.

       We are now ready to deﬁne our desired left-shape matrix L to write our moment matrix
Λ = LQL T in the three-way decomposition.

                                                   329
Proposition 8.5.7. For any shape α with color CP, let α = σ ◦ τ ◦ σ′ , σ and σ′ with color
CP(σ ), CP(σ′ ) satisfy the above property of left/right shape.

Proof. We verify the two conditions for a left-shape. First, if Vσ is incident to some vertex
a ∈ V (σ) \ Vσ via a cross-color edge, there is a path from Uα to a without passing through
Vσ . However, a can be reached via a cross-color edge in Vσ , and by construction, we
require Vσ to be the MVS between Uσ and any vertex reachable from Vσ without using
pure-color edge, including a, and therefore we have arrived at a contradiction. The second
property follows as we choose the candidate leftmost MVS each time before we extend for
cross-color edges.

    Crucial to our three-way factorization is the following left-shape matrix L that we are
ready to deﬁne now,

Deﬁnition 8.5.8 (Color-Left-Shape Matrix L). We deﬁne L = ∑(σ,CP):left-color-shape λ(σ)CP ·
Mσ,CP .


Extension Pieces      As we deﬁne our rainbow MVS subroutine recursively, it is also useful
for us to additionally deﬁne extension pieces/shapes to help us keep track of how a middle
shape arises in the decomposition.

Deﬁnition 8.5.9 (Left/rightwards Rainbow Extension Shape/Piece γrb ). We call a color-
shape γ with color-partition CP a leftwards rainbow extension shape/piece if

   1. Vγ is the unique MVS for γ;

   2. Any vertex in Uγ is in the rainbow component of some vertex in Vγ under CP.

   3. Every vertex is connected to Uγ , and to Vγ .

Taking the shape transpose allows us to deﬁne right-rainbow extension.

                                                 330
Deﬁnition 8.5.10 (Left/rightwards MVS Extension Shape/Piece γmvs ). We call a color-shape
γ with color-partition CP a leftwards MVS extension shape/piece if

   1. Uγ is an MVS for γ;

   2. Any edge in E(γ) incident to Vγ is pure-color under CP.

Proposition 8.5.11 (Decomposition of Middle Shape via Tracking Rainbow-MVS Decom-
position Process). For any shape α with color CP , let α = σ ◦ τ ◦ σ′ be the decomposition from
the above process, τ further admits the following decomposition


           τ = γtmvs
                 l
                     ◦ γtrbl ◦ · · · ◦ γ1mvs ◦ γ1rb ◦ τbase ◦ (γ′ )1rb ◦ (γ′ )1mvs ◦ . . . γtrbr ◦ γtmvs
                                                                                                     r




for some collection of {γirb , (γ′ )rb                                  mvs , ( γ′ )mvs } mvs-extension
                                    i } rainbow-extension shapes and { γi           i

shapes for tl , tr ≤ dsos, and τbase some generic middle shape (i.e. Uτbase , Vτbase are the MVS of τbase ).

Proof. Consider applying the prescribed process of ﬁnding rainbow separators for α. The
base level starts at S L , SR the leftmost and rightmost MVS of α, and the component in
between gives τbase that is a generic middle shape.

    Each time we apply the process, it starts from the given boundary, and ﬁrst extends
to left/right via rainbow-edges and then check for MVS extension. For each iteration of
rainbow-extension, we verify that each piece γ, γ T being factorized out satisﬁes the prop-
erties rainbow-extension. Consider γ a piece obtained from leftwards rainbow-extension.
Firstly, note that Vγ is the unique MVS for γ, as otherwise the prior iteration of ﬁnding
the leftmost MVS can be further moved to the left. Secondly, observe any vertex in Uγ
is in the rainbow component of some vertex in Vγ in the prescribed CP by deﬁnition of
our decomposition process. This gives us the left/rightwards rainbow-extension shape in
each level.

    At each level, notice we further ﬁnd X, Z the component between Uα and the Uγ

                                                       331
boundary, which component we call X (and similarly for Z). The component between the
MVS and UX in X is then further pulled out and put in the middle (similarly for Z), it is
immediate to observe the component in between is also a left/rightwards MVS extension
piece .

   Finally, observe that we have at most dsos levels as each time the candidate boundary
moves to the left/right via MVS-extension piece, at least some vertex is added compared
to the previous MVS-extension. Therefore, we have at most dsos levels of MVS-extensions.




8.5.4 Rainbow MVS Decomposition for Intersection Shapes

Similar to all prior works that utilize the machinery of approximate matrix factorization,
we incur error terms in matrix multiplication of graph matrix of shapes, and we call these
error terms "intersection shapes". We refer reader to [JPR+ 22, JPRX23] for a more thorough
introduction of intersection terms, while we focus on the adaptation of discussion and
terminologies therein to our setting.



Preliminaries for Intersection Shapes         In the above decomposition for proper shape, we
intend to write a shape α = σ ◦ τ ◦ σ′ via matrix multiplication of


                                       Mα = Mσ · Mτ · Mσ ′


However, this equality does not hold exactly, and instead we encounter intersection shapes
deﬁned as the following.


Deﬁnition 8.5.12 (General Intersection Shape/Proﬁle). We call a shape τP a general intersec-
tion shape if it can be obtained from the following

                                                332
     1. It is associated with a t-way products of graph matrices from α1 , α2 , . . . , αt such that each
           consecutive shape is composable (sharing with the same boundary condition);


     2. Additionally associated with it is a intersection proﬁle PτP that speciﬁes how each vertex in
           V (αi ) share the same vertex label in [n], i.e. intersect with vertices in other parts of V (α j )
           for j ̸= i while preserving local injectivity within each piece/shape.


         With some abuse of notation, we may also refer to the intersection proﬁle as τP . Note
that this also allows us to view proper shape as an intersection shape as well with trivial
intersection proﬁle.

         It is intuitive to picture P(τP ) as additionally drawing edges between intersected ver-
tices in σ ◦ τ ◦ σ′ , as captured by the following example.


Example 8.5.13. The following is an example of intersection pattern, where the orange curves
specify how diﬀerent set of vertices in diﬀerent parts may intersect by receiving the same label in
[ n ].




                                                  S(L)       S(R)
                                            L                       R
                                                         M
                                  U                                        V

                                       Figure 8.9: Intersection Patten


         With intersection shapes considered, we have instead the following equality from
matrix multiplication of graph matrices that extend beyond 3-way product,



                                                         333
Proposition 8.5.14. For composable shapes α1 , α2 , . . . , αt ,


                              Mα1 · Mα2 · . . . Mα t =           ∑               MτP
                                                         τP =(α1 ◦α2 ◦···◦αk )
                                                          intersection proﬁle


Proof. The proposition follows immediately by a direct expansion of the LHS, and note
that each term is a shape under some intersection proﬁle that speciﬁes how vertices
intersect.

    As we shift from proper shapes to improper shapes, a technical issue that comes up is
that we have multi-edge in the deﬁnition for graph matrix, and this incurs some necessary
adjustment for graph matrix norm bounds, captured by the notion of isolated vertices.

Deﬁnition 8.5.15 (Isolated vertex). Given a (possibly with intersection) shape α, we call a
vertex v ∈ V (α) \ (Uα ∪ Vα ) an isolated vertex if it is not incident to any edge in E(α) with
odd-multiplicity.

    We are now ready to wrap up the preliminaries for our intersection shapes, and de-
scribe the decomposition for them. A priori, these are error terms in the approximate
matrix factorization and they are of small norm. However, similar to how matrix factor-
ization is needed, intersection shapes are also of small norm only if we zoom into the
"correct" eigenspace (i.e. decomposition).


Recap of Prior Strategy        From the previous works, a three-way decomposition of ribbons
L ◦ M ◦ R may incur intersections from the ribbon boundary, and the usual strategy
employed is to factor out the the uninersected part from the left and right, and identify the
diagonal being charged to as Uγ the leftmost MVS separating UL from VR ∪ intersection,
and Vγ′ the rightmost MVS separating VR from UL ∪ intersection. This ultimately comes
down to verifying the shape γ ◦ τ ◦ γ′ can be appropriately charged where γ is the shape

                                                   334
                                                           ′
obtained from edges between Uγ and VL in L, and γ′ the shape obtained from edges
between UR and Vγ′ .



Upgrade to Rainbow Separators via Modiﬁed Start          For our case, we employ the strategy
inspired by the above, and incorporate it to our Rainbow MVS subroutine for proper shape
by making the likely anticipated change to our initialization,


Instead of starting from the left/rightmost MVS separating Uα and Vα , we start building
S L from the leftmost MVS separating Uα and Vα ∪ Int(α), and analogously start building
                SR from the rightmost MVS separating Vα and Uα ∪ Int(α) .


   With the modiﬁed starting point, the recursive stage remains the same as in the pro-
cess described in section 8.5.2: we alternately apply the below operations of Rainbow-
Extension Operation and MVS-Extension Operation (including their updates) on S L and
SR , until each converges, i.e. S L does not change after a consecutive application of both
extension operations, and analogously for SR .

   Crucial to our recursive scheme is to notice that by further decomposing intersection
shape, we are eﬀectively factorizing out the non-intersected component in the three-way
product. Consider the three-way product of σ ◦ τ ◦ σ′ with some intersection incurred to
ψ, applying (S L , SR )-decomposition from our intersection-operation, we may factor out
the intersecting part γ from σ, and γ′ from σ′ , and write


                                 ψ = (σ − γ) ◦ τP ◦ (σ − γ)


where τP is the result of intersection from γ, τ, γ′ . Formally, for an intersection shape from
the three-way product, we deﬁne its corresponding γ and γ′ shape within σ and σ′ via
the following process.

                                             335
  Finding Intersection Shapes γ and γ′
  For an intersection shape ψ obtained from three-way product of (σ, τ, σ′ ) , and with
  (S L , SR ) identiﬁed from the intersection decomposition operation, we deﬁne shape γ
  and γ′ as the following.

     1. γ shape is deﬁned as the following: let Uγ := S L , and Vγ = Vσ = Uτ . Consider
        E(σ − γ) be the set of edges in E(σ ) that can be reached from Uσ without passing
        through S L = Uγ , and we deﬁne E(γ) := E(σ) \ E(σ − γ). Under this deﬁnition,
        we follow the convention such that edges between the vertices in E(Uγ ) are put
        in E(γ).

     2. γ′ shape is deﬁned analogously as the following: let Uγ := Uσ′ = Vτ , and Vγ′ =
        SR . Consider E(σ′ − γ′ ) be the set of edges in E(σ′ ) that can be reached from Vσ′
        without passing through SR = Vγ′ , and we deﬁne E(γ′ ) := E(σ′ ) \ E(σ′ − γ′ ).

     3. Additionally, we call (γ, τ, γ′ )’s intersection result τP as a three-way intersection.

     4. For each γ, γ′ shape, we additionally associate with each of them Int(γ) and
        Int(γ′ ) the set of vertices that intersect with some other vertex in the intersection
        proﬁle prescribed by ψ.


Remark 8.5.16. Though we did not make the coloring-dependence explicit, it should be noted that
each shape discussed above is a color-shape that comes with some color-partition.


Deﬁnition 8.5.17 (Intersection-left/right-shape). We call each color-shape (γ, CPγ ), (γ′ , CPγ′ )
obtained from the above process a left/right intersection (color-)shape.


   Useful to our analysis of intersection term is the following structural property of
intersection left/right shape.


                                                336
Proposition 8.5.18 (γ-shape property). For any left-intersection γ-shape obtained from the
above process, it satisﬁes the following properties:


   1. γ is a left-color-shape, i.e. Vγ is the unique MVS, and additionally a color MVS that is only
      incident to pure-color edge;


   2. Uγ is a color MVS separating Uγ and Int(γ) ∪ Vγ : in other words, it is the result of
      leftwards extensions from the MVS separating Uγ and Int(γ) ∪ Vγ .


Analogous property hold for right-intersection shape γ′ by considering its shape transpose into a
left-shape.


Proof. The ﬁrst property holds from σ being a color-left-shape, hence Vσ = Vγ is a unique
MVS that also satisﬁes the coloring constraint. The second property follows from our
construction of S L from the decomposition process for intersection shape.


Remark 8.5.19. As with previous SoS lower bounds using graph matrices, a key observation is
that the intersection operation and the intersection term decomposition operation are unaﬀected by
replacing a diﬀerent shape of σ − γ and σ′ − γ′ provided they share the same boundary condition
of Vσ−γ = S L = Uγ and Uσ−γ = SR = Vγ′ .


    With the deﬁnition of γ shape, we are also able to deﬁne the collection of intersection
shapes that come up in our analysis obtained after factoring out the non-intersection part.




Deﬁnition 8.5.20 (Factored Intersection Shape/Proﬁle with Level-t). We call a shape τP a
general intersection shape if it can be obtained from the following


   1. It is associated with a t-way products of shapes from γt ◦ γt−1 ◦ τ ◦ γ1′ ◦ ◦˙ γt′ such that each
      consecutive shape is composable (sharing with the same boundary condition);

                                                 337
   2. For each level i ∈ [t] γi and γi′ are intersection left/right-shapes respectively, moreover, at
         least one of them is non-trivial;


   3. τ is a proper middle shape (i.e. Uτ and Vτ are obtained under left/right-extensions from the
         leftmost/rightmost MVS of τ).


   4. Additionally associated with it is a intersection proﬁle PτP that speciﬁes how each vertex in
         V (αi ) share the same vertex label in [n], i.e. intersect with vertices in other parts of V (α j )
         for j ̸= i while preserving local injectivity within each piece/shape.

8.6 Combinatorial Charging Analysis for Non-intersected

          Shapes

For clarity of our presentation, we focus on the dense regime of G (n, 21 ) in the subsequent
sections, and we defer the necessary changes to the appendix for the application to the
sparse regime of G (n, nd ) for d = o (n).

    To get started, recall from proposition 8.5.11, a middle shape τ ∈ Q0 admits the
following decomposition

                                               341
           τ = At ◦ Xt ◦ At−1 ◦ Xt−1 ◦ · · · ◦ A1 ◦ X1 ◦ Y ◦ Z1 ◦ B1 ◦ · · · ◦ Zt′ ◦ Bt′

where we abbreviate the color-partition dependence of each shape as it is clear each is a
color-shape, each Ai , Bj is a left/right-rainbow-extension shape in the decomposition, and
Xi , Zj is a left/right-MVS-extension shape and Y is a base middle shape.


Motivation for Slack Function           Finally, we handle the discrepancy between a single
shape and summing over shapes as ultimately we would be interested in the summation
of shapes while the above discussion focus on single-shape. To cope with the summation
over color-shapes, we introduce the slack function for each shape deﬁned as the following.



Deﬁnition 8.6.1 (Slack function). For each shape (τ, CP), we deﬁne


                               slack(τ, CP) = |λτ,CP | · ∥Mτ,CP ∥


   For intuition, one should think of slack(·) as a priori some factor less than 1 we want
to control via combinatorial analysis. Moreover, we ideally want to upper bound it away
from 1 so that we continue to get a gap after summing over all shapes. This is the main
task of our combinatorial analysis, culminated at the following lemma,

Lemma 8.6.2 (Main lemma for Q0 ). For any non-trivial shape (τ, CP) ∈ Q0 , we have


                                   ∥λ(τ )CP · Mτ,CP ∥ ≤ o (1)


in our regime of k. Formally, we show


                               ∥λ(τ )CP · Mτ,CP ∥ ≤ slack(τp , CP)

                   k                       k

Factorization of coeﬃcient function into pieces          We now deﬁne piece-function for λ(·)
in the above fashion for the weight function. A priori, this is less immediate as the factor
Ev may span across pieces. For example, in the simplest case of τ = X ◦ Y ◦ Z, i.e. we have
one level of rainbow extension via X and Z starting from the MVS of UY and VY . Since
UY , VY are only guaranteed to be MVS instead of rainbow MVS, a ﬂoating component
incident to UY , VY may have edges across two pieces. However, such dependence can be
controlled once we observe each rainbow component cannot stretch "too far" beyond each
rainbow-extension piece.


Claim 8.6.5. Each rainbow component may have edges in at most two adjacent pieces beyond
the base level. In other words, any rainbow component with edges in Xi (resp. Zi ), its edges is
completely contained in both Xi and Ai+1 (resp. Zi and Bi+1 ) for i ̸= 0. For the base level, we may
have rainbow component with edges in both X1 , Y, Z1 but there are no more pieces that contain its
edges.


Proof. For any rainbow component C with edges in Xi that additionally has edges in
Ai+1 , any rainbow edge incident to U Ai+1 is contained in Ai+1 , that said, C as a rainbow
component cannot have edges to the left of U Ai+1 . The same argument applies for Z shape
by ﬂipping the boundary.

k · (k − 1) · · · · · (k − |V (C )|) many such rainbow colorings, and we apply approximation

Proof. For starters, we remind the reader that each rainbow component by deﬁnition
includes no isolated vertex after removal of pure-edge while we focus on rainbow compo-
nent with edges for this section as isolated vertex trivially gives Ev = 1 here. We prove
this by processing rainbow component in conn(τ ). The base case holds trivially with no
rainbow component added.

    Let C be a rainbow component in τ, by claim 8.6.5, there are at most two adjacent pieces
containing its edges Xi and Ai−1 suppose it appears to the left of Y if not intersecting Y, or
it may have edges contained in X1 , Y, Z1 . We ﬁrst observe that in the case of C having all
its edges contained in a single piece of { A j , Bj , Y } , the above is immediate as the LHS gets
a factor of |V (C )| − 1 and the RHS picks up the same factor from the piece that contains
the component C. Analogously, this further holds for component C that appears in Xt (or
Zt piece) yet not incident to VXt (or UZt respectively).

    In the case C is incident to VXt for some Xt (for some t > 1), its vertices may be

                                                              349
decomposed into a collection of rainbow components in Xt and At−1 . Observe that
V (C ) ∩ V ( At−1 ) is a collection of at least 1 single rainbow component, writing RHS(C ) as
the gain from component C on the RHS, we pick up a factor of


                RHS A (C ) ≤ |V (C ) ∩ V ( At−1 )| − 1 ≤ |V (C ) ∩ V ( At−1 )| − 1


from the edge factors in At−1 . On the other hand, from Xt , we pick up a factor of


          RHSX (C ) = |V (C ) ∩ V ( Xt ) \ VXt | = |V (C ) ∩ V ( Xt )| − |V (C ) ∩ U At−1 |


where we use VXt = U At−1 . Summing the above gives us the gain on the RHS being


RHS(C ) = RHS A (C ) + RHSY (C ) ≤ |V (C ) ∩ V ( At−1 )| − 1 + |V (C ) ∩ V ( Xt )| − |V (C ) ∩ U At−1 | = |V (C )|


where we use |V (C ) ∩ V ( At−1 )| + |V (C ) ∩ V ( Xt )| = |V (C )| − |V (C ) ∩ U At−1 |. And note
that the LHS factor is exactly |V (C )| − 1 and this proves the case when C is a rainbow
component that does not touch upon the base piece.

   For C touching upon the base piece, the pieces of focus are now X1 , Y, Z1 . Notice
V (C ) ∩ V (Y ) under CP(Y ) is a collection of at least a single rainbow component, which
gives us a factor of at most


                                RHSY (C ) ≤ |V (C ) ∩ V (Y )| − 1


from the piece Y. Combining with the factors in X1 , Z1 , we have a total of at most


      RHS(C ) ≤ |V (C ) ∩ V ( X1 ) \ VX1 | + |V (C ) ∩ V ( Z1 ) \ UZ1 | + |V (C ) ∩ V (Y )| − 1


Since |V (C ) ∩ V ( X1 ) \ VX1 | + |V (C ) ∩ V ( Z1 ) \ UZ1 | + |V (C ) ∩ V (Y )| = |V (C )|, we can

                                                350
bound the above again by |V (C )| − 1 as desired.




      Finally, the above shows the Ev component of both sides holds as the desired inequality,
and for the vertex-factor of 1k , notice that this component holds with the usual strategy of
splitting the factor into two pieces across the boundaries of pieces,

      1. Each vertex in V (τ ) \ Uτ ∪ Vτ comes with a factor of 1k : it either has its factor split
         across two adjacent pieces, in which case we pick up ( √1 )2 = 1k , or it is a vertex that
                                                                              k
         appears in the interior of some piece, which is a whole factor of 1k ;

      2. Vertices in Uτ ∆Vτ comes with a factor of √1 : it either starts from the left (or right)
                                                              k
        piece and gets popped out of the boundary ultimately, and picks up the correspond-
         ing factor √1 from the piece it gets popped out;
                        k


      3. Vertices in Uτ ∩ Vτ remain dormant throughout the decomposition, and does not
         receive any factor.

Proof to proposition 8.6.12. Oserve that each rainbow component has a path to Uτ and Vτ ,
hence we may consider a traversal from Uτ to assign the 1k factors: in particular, each
vertex reached some rainbow edge within the rainbow component receives a 1k decay. We
then observe the vertices outside Uτ ∩ Vτ receive the factor on both sides in G and G ′ . For
vertices in Uτ ∩ Vτ , G ′ does not assign any vertex decay for vertices, while for rainbow
                                                                                 |V (C )|

Proof to proposition 8.6.13. We prove the proposition by showing


                ∑         (|V ( Gi )| − 1) ≥ |CP(Y )| − |CP(UY )| = |CP(Y ) \ CP(UY )| ,
            Gi ∈G(Y,CP)


and similarly


                ∑         (|V ( Gi )| − 1) ≥ |CP(Y )| − |CP(VY )| = |CP(Y ) \ CP(VY )| .
            Gi ∈G(Y,CP)


The main proposition then follows by taking the average of the above two lines. We now
focus on the inequality restricted on UY . Unpacking the deﬁnition, we remind the reader

                                                            353
that |CP(Y ) \ CP(UY )| is simply the number of colors that appear in Y but not in UY .
Consider a traversal from UY , we bound the contribution to the RHS, a.k.a., the new color
explored, in each rainbow component. In particular, we show that we can assign a factor
of 1 on the LHS for each vertex outside S L that takes a new color in the traversal.

      We now recall that for Y, this may be decomposed into a collection of rainbow com-
ponents in G connected via pure-color edge to UY (and equivalently VY ) since there is
no ﬂoating component in τ. For any rainbow-component C incident to UY , any such
component contributes at most V (C ) − 1 new colors to the RHS, while we pick up a factor
of |V (C )| − 1. For each rainbow component C not incident to UY , it must be reached via
some pure-color edge, that said, it contributes a factor of |V (C )| − 1 factor to the LHS via
its coeﬃcient, and again it contributes at most |V (C )| − 1 colors. Summing over rainbow
components in G(Y, CP) proves the desired inequality.

Proof. Partition the vertices according to their color, for each group of vertices with color c,
we count the contribution of this group to both sides of the equation. Process the vertices
of color c in the following order,

   1. If some vertex v in with color c appears in both of U, V boundary, the group con-
      tributes 0 to the LHS, and we pick up at least 0 on the RHS via this vertex;

   2. Otherwise, if some vertex v in c appears in one of the U, V boundaries but not both,

                                              356
        we get a factor of 12 on the LHS, and via this vertex v’s contribution to the RHS, we
        pick up a factor of 21 .


      3. If no vertex of color c appears in U, V boundary while some vertex v takes color c
        outside the boundary, we get a +1 from the LHS, and again a +1 on the RHS.


      4. Any extra vertex with color c can only bring non-negative contribution to the RHS.


Processing all color c in I yields the desired.

8.7 Combinatorial Charging Analysis for Intersection Shapes

8.7.1 Analysis Set-up

We now introduce some lemmas to set up for our charging analysis, and our starting point
is a piecewise decomposition for γ and γ′ shape in our three-way intersection.


Piecewise Decomposition for γ-Shape For our analysis, identical to our decomposition
of middle shape into various pieces via left/rightwards extensions, we apply the same
decomposition for γ shape.

Proposition 8.7.1. For each (color) left-intersection γ shape, we can decompose it as


                            γ = A t ◦ X t ◦ · · · ◦ A 2 ◦ X2 ◦ A 1 ◦ X1 ◦ Y


where Ai is a left-rainbow-extension shape, and Xi is a left-MVS-extension shape, and Y is a
generic γ-shape such that

   1. UY is an MVS separating UY and Int(Y ) ∪ VY ;

                                                  360
   2. Y is an left shape (i.e. VY is the unique MVS).

Proof. This is analogous to the decomposition of middle shape into pieces, and the base
shape γbase satisﬁes the generic property as it is starting from the leftmost MVS separating
Uσ and Vσ ∪ Int(σ ) = Vγ ∪ Int(γ).

    Next, we recall deﬁne the weight function and coeﬃcient function extension pieces
deﬁned as the following (see deﬁnition 8.6.8).
    For γ ◦ τ ◦ γ′ a tuple of color-shapes that intersect to τP , let UY be the leftmost MVS
separating Uγ and Vγ ∪ Int(γ), and VY ′ be the rightmost MVS separating Vγ′ and Uγ′ ∪
Int(γ′ ), i.e. these are the original separator in the decomposition process before any
left/rightwards extension. Notice the component between Uγ and UY , between Vγ′ and
VY are untouched by the intersection, that said, we can factor these components out by
ﬁnding Lγ ( and respectively Rγ′ ) be the subset of edges in E(γ) (and E(γ′ )) reachable
from Uγ (and Vγ′ ) without passing through UY and VY ′ . This allows us to deﬁne τeP as the
shape obtained from τP yet with the same intersection pattern,

   1. τeP is obtained from intersecting vertices in Y ◦ τ ◦ Y ′ according to the intersection
      pattern in τP ;

   2. UτeP = UY , and VτeP = VY ′ .

In other words, this is the usual intersection shape before any left/rightwards extensions
in γ and γ′ .

γ ◦ τ ◦ γ′ a tuple of color-shapes that intersect to τP , let τeP be the result of intersection in Y ◦ τ ◦ Y ′

Claim 8.7.7. For Y a left-shape,


                                       |VY | + | Idc (UY )| ≤ |UY |


Proof. Recall that Idc (UY ) ⊆ UY \ VY is the set of edges not incident to any edge in Y, any
path from VY to Idc (UY ) is trivially blocked. Next we observe that VY is also an MVS (in
fact, unique) separating UY \ Idc (UY ) and VY : otherwise, suppose T ̸= VY is such an
MVS with | T | ≤ |VY |, T is also a separator for Y and we have a contradiction that Y is a
left-shape in which VY is the unique MVS. Since UY \ Idc (UY ) is also a separator separating
UY \ Idc (UY ) and VY , we have


                                        |VY | ≤ |UY \ Idc (UY )|


Rearranging gives us the desired.

8.8 Handling the Kernel

8.8.1 Overview of the Kernel Analysis

In this section, we address the kernel issue in LQL T factorization. In the previous section,
we focus on terms that appear in Q without intersections. That said, in our context, since
we have a non-trivial kernel coming from the coloring constraint, it turns out that there are
terms that cannot be charged to the diagonal of Id by a magnitude argument. In particular,
instead of getting Iddsos on the diagonal, we have ( Id − kJ )dsos .


A Candidate Kernel from the Trivial Term in Q

Lemma 8.8.1. Following the LQL T decomposition of Λ, the trivial shape (edgeset being ∅) appears
in Q as ( I − Jkk )⊗u for U = V and |U | = |V |.

Proof. Let Q∅ := ⟨ Q, ∅⟩ be the matrix of coeﬃcient of empty edge-set in Q where ⟨·, ·⟩
denotes the Fourier inversion operator. In other words, for any entry (S, β S ), ( T, β T ),


                           Q∅ [(S, β S ), ( T, β T )] := ⟨∅, Q[(S, β S ), ( T, β T )]⟩ .

                                                       372
    It suﬃces for us to show the entry of Q∅ [(U, βU ), (U, β′U )] = (1 − 1k )u · (− 1k )q . for u
the number of vertices with the same color in both βU and β′U , and q be the number of
vertices with diﬀerent colors under β, β′ . For convenience, we denote any such entry by
F (u, q).

Claim 8.8.2. For any u, q ≥ 0, F (u, q) satisﬁes the following recurrence

                                         a≤u,b≤q         

with base case F (0, 0) = 1, F (1, 0) = 1 − 1k , and F (0, 1) = − 1k .


Proof. The base case is equivalent to looking at Q[∅, ∅] and we have 1 from the trivial shape,
and observe that there is no intersection term at this entry, i.e., Qi [∅, ∅] = 0 and hence the
base case. The further condition of F (1, 0) and F (0, 1) holds as ⟨ Q1 [(i, _), (i, _)], ∅⟩ = − 1k ,
while F (1, 0) contains the extra trivial shape without intersection from Q0 [(i, a), (i, a)] = 1.

    For the general case, observe that the term 1[q = 0] controls the contribution from Q0 ,
i.e. we only pick non-intersected trivial shape from Q0 when every vertex has the same
color in both coloring. For contribution from Qi for i > 0, we case on the outmost level of
intersections,


   1. Let a be the number of trivial intersection from vertices with the same color, and b
      be the number of trivial intersection from vertices with diﬀerent color;


   2. Since the pure-color/cross-color vertices are ﬁxed to start with, there are simply (ua)
      and (qb) ways of choosing intersection vertices at the outer level, provided a + b > 0
      (i.e., some vertex intersection happens);


   3. Notice that each vertex intersection gives us a factor of 1k along with a (−1) sign
      for any level of intersection; once choosing the action of the outmost level, the

                                                    373
      contribution is then multiplied by the coeﬃcient of the subsequent level, given by
      F (u − a, q − b) by our hypothesis. Alternatively, given any term contributing to
      F (u − a, q − b), there are (ua) · (qb) of picking it to contribute F (u, q) by padding non-
      trivial intersection at the outmost level.




   Finally, inducting on u, q such that F (u, q) = (1 − 1k )u · (− 1k )q , the we complete the
proof to the lemma by the next two equalities from the Binomial Theorem.

Candidate Kernel of Q: a ﬁrst try          Before we formally show the kernel vectors, it is
enticing at the ﬁrst attempts to suspect the following subspace be the kernel of Q as given
by the trivial shape on the diagonal block corresponding to colorings of the same set of
vertices.

   To get started, we deﬁne the coloring-vector that naturally comes up when working
with the color-indexed moment matrix,


Deﬁnition 8.8.3 (Coloring-vector). For any dsos ∈ N, we deﬁne coloring vector as the vector
in the appropriate dimension for the corresponding color-moment matrix, i.e., each vector’s entry
is indexed by


   • S ⊆ [n], and |S| ≤ dsos;


   • β S : S → [k]: the coloring for S.

                                                                          n]×[k ]
With some abuse of notation, we consider this vector to be of dimension ([≤ dsos ).

Deﬁnition 8.8.4 (Candidate kernel of 1(S,β S )∪T ). For any set ∅ ̸= T ⊆ [n], we deﬁne KT =

    2. 1T = ∑ β T :V (T )→[k] 1T,β T , i.e., the vector with non-zero entry 1 on any coloring of T.

    1. This is a coloring-vector with non-zero entries indexed by (S ∪ T, β S ∪ β T ) for any coloring
        β T : T → [ k ],

    2. and each non-zero entry is 1.

For notational clarity, we also write 1(S,β S ),T = 1(S,β S )∪T to emphasize that S are the vertices with
ﬁxed colors while we are summing over colors of T, consistent to our deﬁnition of kernel partition.

Example 8.8.5. Plugging in S = ∅ and T = {i } gives us the k-dimensional all-1 vector on color
of i.

    However, this natural guess turns out to be false, unlike the lower bound for deg-2 SoS,
it can be veriﬁed that v = ei,1 ⊗ 1 j , i.e. the vector v that has all 1-entry on v[(i, 1), ( j, c j )]
and 0 everywhere else is not exactly in the kernel of Q. See the following diagram for
illustration. Another way to see this issue is that there are also additional truncation
terms in the ﬁnal matrix Q that are of small magnitude but present, hence we need a more
robust way of handling the kernel.
    This motivates us to adjust the proof strategy for the kernel as the following inspired
by the kernel trick from [GJJ+ 20b]. Our proof strategy is two-step,

    1. Identify the Kernel of Q;

   2. "Filling in" the kernel of Q so that the ﬁnal matrix has genuine identity on the
       diagonal as PSD mass to enable a magnitude argument.

     Recall from deﬁnition 8.5.8 that L the left-shape matrix with rows indexed by (Uσ , βU )
and columns indexed by (Vσ , β V ), we next show that L does not have non-trivial right
kernel, and therefore, for any vector v ∈ Ker (Λ), we also have L T v ∈ Ker ( Q).


Lemma 8.8.10. L does not have non-trivial right kernel, i.e., for any vector v ̸= 0, we have Lv ̸= 0;
similarly, L does not have non-trivial left-kernel, i.e. L T v ̸= 0 for any v ̸= 0.


Proof. We ﬁrst prove that L does not have non-trivial right-kernel. For any v ̸= 0, consider
the minimal support of v, i.e., the index (S, β S ) such that v[(S, β S )] ̸= 0 for the smallest
|S|, and we claim that Lv[(S, β S )] = v[(S, β S )] ̸= 0.
     To see this observe that L[(S, β S ), (S, β S )] = ( √1 )|S| , and moreover, for any T with
                                                                  k
| T | < |S|, we have v[( T, β T )] = 0 for any β T by our minimal assumption. That said, it
suﬃces for us to examine the entries of L[(S, β S ), ( T, β T )] for | T | ≥ |S|. Note that for
| T | = |S| yet S ̸= T, there is no non-zero term as the only left shape with |Uσ | = |Vσ | is
the identity shape; on the other hand, for | T | > |S|, there is no left-shape of this form as
for any left shape σ we need to have |Uσ | ≥ |Vσ |. Therefore, we have Lv ̸= 0 for non-zero
v.

     Similarly, for L T v, consider the largest index |S| such that v[(S, β S )] ̸= 0, and observe
that L T v[(S, β S )] = v[(S, β S )] ̸= 0.


Claim 8.8.11 (L T K (Λ) ⊆ Ker ( Q)). For any v(S,β S ),T ∈ K (Λ),


                                             Q L T v(S,β S ),T = 0 .
                                               | {z }
                                                 :=u(S,β ),T

Proof. By claim 8.8.9, we know
                                              Λv(S,β S ),T = 0 ,

and since Λ = LQL T , we have


                              LQL T v(S,β S ),T = LQ · u(S,β S ),T = 0.


By lemma 8.8.10, L does not have non-trivial left-kernel, hence we have


                                             Q · u(S,β S ),T = 0 .


Moreover, L does not have non-trivial right-kernel, thus u(S,β S ),T ̸= 0 and is in the kernel
of Q.



PSD Analysis via Filling in the Kernel                     Now that we have identiﬁed the kernel of
matrix Q, we note that we may still apply a magnitude-bound based argument except on
a closely related matrix to show the PSDness of Q.


Claim 8.8.12. For any symmetric matrix M with kernel Ker ( M), M is PSD if

identifying the right choice of ui ∈ Ker ( Q), however, this turns out to be a surprisingly
challenging task as K constructed from the natural kernel-vector (coloring-vector) contains
extra large-norm components that warrant extra care. Towards this end, analogous to the
recursive factorization scheme via LQL T , we iteratively identify the kernel-vector such
that the resulting matrix is amenable to a magnitude-bound analysis for PSDness. This is
the focus of our next subsection, and we give a summary at the end of this section to wrap
up our kernel analysis.




8.8.2 Overview for Kernel-Factorization

Recall our deﬁnition of kernel partition deﬁned from the overview section,


Deﬁnition 8.8.13 (Kernel Partition). Given a set B ⊆ [n], we call S( B) ∪ T ( B) a kernel partition
for B if vertices in S( B) receives some ﬁxed color, and T ( B) are the vertices whose colors are being
summed over. In other words, a kernel partition is a kernel-partition with partial-coloring except
with β S( B) the ﬁxed colors on S( B) unspeciﬁed.


Deﬁnition 8.8.14 (Kernel matrix KS∪T for Kernel-Partition). For any kernel partition S ∪ T ⊆
[n] such that S (possibly empty) are the vertices with color ﬁxed to some β S , and T ̸= ∅ the vertices
without color. We deﬁne
                                         KS∪T := ⊗i∈S∪T Ki

where Ki = Idk is a k × k identity matrix for i ∈ S and Ki = − Jkk for i ∈ T is the the all-1 matrix
of dimension k × k scaled by a factor of − 1k ,

    In other words, this is a coloring matrix of dimension ([n] × [k])|S∪T | × ([n] × [k ])|S∪T | , and

                                                  381
has non-zero entry at

                                                                              1
                          KS∪T [(S ∪ T, β S ∪ β T ), (S ∪ T, β S ∪ β′T )] = (− )|T | .
                                                                              k

for any β S : S → [k] and β T , β′T : T → [k]. .

    Note that K(S,β S )∪T corresponds to the damage of identity in Q due to intersection
terms inside T (where S are the non-intersected vertices with ﬁxed colors as β S for any β S )
. The goal of this section is to identify a construction of kernel-ﬁlling vectors f S∪T for S ∪ T
such that L T f S∪T can be used as a proxy to generate matrix KS∪T . Formally, we construct
KQ as the following from deﬁned f S∪T vectors,

Deﬁnition 8.8.15 (Construction of KQ from Kernel-Filling Vectors). Given a collection of
coloring vectors { f (S,β S ),T } , we deﬁne KQ to be a coloring matrix of corresponding dimension for
dsos

    Recall that
                                  Ktrivial =       ∑            ∑            KS(W )∪T (W )

for some matrix Knontrivial := KQ − Ktrivial that may be ultimately viewed as an error term in

scheme for kernel-vectors.

   On a high level, our recursive construction scheme starts from the natural kernel-
                       √ |S|
vector of r(S,β S ),T = k (−1(S,β S ) + 1S(,β S ),T ), and note that this gives a sequence of
(kernel) right-shapes in KQ with Vσ = |S ∪ T | (ignoring the symmetry, otherwise we have
a collection of left shapes obtained by the transpose of the above as well). However, this is
not the correct construction as the collection of right-shapes may contain large norm terms.
And the crux of our recursive construction scheme is to show that they are again lying in
the kernel of Q, and can be removed with some other careful choice of kernel-factors.

   En route to establishing this, our crude goal, as a guiding heuristic from observing
the structural property of large norm shape obtained under the construction from the
natural kernel vectors (after summing overall S, T ⊆ [n]), is to ﬁnd a collection of vectors
f in the kernel of Q corresponding to each kernel partition such that the terms (i.e.
shapes) appearing in L T f (S,β S ),T admit the following structural characterization (informally,
ignoring color dependence):


   1. Each term is a right-shape σ with Vσ = S ∪ T;


   2. S is an MVS that separates Uσ and S;


   3. Each vertex in S ∪ T either has path to Uσ , or is a component connected to T (Vσ ) but
      not connected to Uσ ∪ S(Vσ ) ;

                                               383
And to ensure f (S,β S ),T lies in the kernel of Q, our strategy is to write f as a linear
combination of our kernel-basis vectors r(S,β S ),T . We adopt a recursive construction for
f (S,β S ),T , and take


        f (S,β S )∪T := r(S,β S )∪T −        ∑            f (SW ,β S )∪TW · R[(SW , β SW ) ∪ TW , (S, β S ) ∪ T ]
                                                                    W       |               {z                 }
                                        (SW ,β SW )∪ TW
                                                                                         coeﬃcient


for some coeﬃcient-function R[(SW , β SW ) ∪ TW , (S, β S ) ∪ T ] to be deﬁned momentarily.

    Concretely, we will take the coeﬃcient function R[(SW , β SW ) ∪ TW , (S, β S ) ∪ T ] to be
a sum of right-shapes that is additionally oblivious of the color in TW . For intuition,
consider the following example. Note the oval boundaries on the right corresponding to
S and S + T are intended to emphasize the kernel-partition in inspection.

Example 8.8.16. Consider the kernel from ﬁxing vertex i to have color 1, and summing over vertex
j’s colors. This can be written as 1(i,1),j − 1(i,1) in our language. The multiplication with coeﬃcient

Description of Kernel Shape for KQ                  To motivate our recursive scheme for constructing
the vectors, it is important that we understand what KQ is to start with, and consider
its expansion in the graph matrix basis. For simplicity, we restrict our attention to the
                      eQ obtained by f (·) ⊗ 1(·) alone. For starters, we observe that any
unsymmetrized version K
term in L T f (S,β S )∪T is a right shape σ with Vσ = S ∪ T.

   Consider the contribution of a ﬁxed right shape σ to KQ after summing over labels
for its right boundary. Formally, putting back the color-dependence, for any color-right-
shape (σ, CP) with Vσ = S ∪ T with β S color of S ﬁxed, its corresponding graph matrix of

∑S,T ⊆[n] ∑ β S Mσ,CP [_, S ∪ T ] ⊗ 1(S,β S ),T = MK (σ) for the following kernel shape K (σ).

Deﬁnition 8.8.18 (Kernel-shape K (σ, CP)). Given a right-shape σ with kernel-partition on
Vσ = S(Vσ ) ∪ T (Vσ ), we deﬁne its corresponding kernel shape K (σ, CP) as the following,


   1. We start with a shape σ and set E(K (σ)) = E(σ ) and UK (σ) := Uσ ;


   2. Include each vertex in S(Vσ ) into VK (σ) ;


   3. The coloring of vertices from V (σ) satisfy constraints from the color-partition of CP;


   4. For each vertex v in T (Vσ ), create an extra copy of the vertex v′ and connect it via a dotted
      line, include v′ into VK (σ) where the dotted line corresponds to a color-jump, the two vertices
      connected have the same vertex label in [n] though not necessarily of the same color.

                        ∑         ∑         Mσ,CP [_, S ∪ T ] ⊗ 1(S,β S ),T = MK (σ),CP(σ)

Cancellation of Troublesome Right Shape              In this case, we observe that the particular
kernel-shape K (σ) can be further decomposed as a product of right-shapes. For illustration,
ﬁx the vertex label on the left to be i, we may rewrite σ as the product of σ = σ1 ◦ σ2 . Note
that Uσ1 = {i }.

   Important to us is that σ1 is a shape that satisﬁes our crude heuristic if we consider
the kernel partition for σ1 as S(Vσ1 ) = (i, _) and T (Vσ1 ) = T: in this case, S(Vσ1 ) is indeed
a MVS for Uσ1 = i and S(Vσ1 ) = i! That said, we can expect to ﬁnd kernel-vector of the
smaller size (reduced from 3 vertices to 2) on {i, T } to generate the particular σ1 under
L T f (i,_),T ) and view σ2 as a coeﬃcient!

   At this point, we remark that the above cancellation strategy is essentially asking for a
further factorization of the component that makes the shape large norm, i.e., this exactly
what approximate factorization via MVS is intended to achieve! Thus, we want to ﬁnd a
decomposition scheme for right shape that allows us to show the bĳection. Another issue
that we ignore so far is that we need to put back the color-dependence as our left-shape
matrix L is consist of color-shape in this setting. This is formalized via the following
decomposition scheme for right shape.




8.8.3 Irreducible Decomposition of Right Shape

We now describe a decomposition procedure that applies to any shape σ with kernel
partition on Vσ = S ∪ T. Our goal is to identify σ = σA ◦ σB such that VσA = UσB = SW ∪ TW
for some TW ⊆ T, and moreover, TW is not incident to any edge in σB .

                                                      388
Finding Kernel (Rainbow) Separator (aka. Kernel Right Shape Decomposition)
Given a color-shape σ with color-partition CPσ such that (σ, CPσ ) is a (color-)right
shape in L T , (i.e. σ is a right shape and any edge incident to Uσ is a pure-color edge
under CP) and Vσ is additionally partitioned into Vσ = S(Vσ ) ∪ T (Vσ ), we deﬁne its
irreducible rainbow separator and decomposition (σ, CPσ ) = (σA , CP A ) ◦ (σB , CPB )
for σA , σB both color-right-shapes as the following:

  1. Initialize the shape B = σ with edges E( B) = E(σ ), and UB = Uσ , VB = Vσ .

  2. Apply alternately the following operation of Rightwards MVS Subtraction or Right-
     wards Rainbow Extension, and Update B Operation until B converges (i.e. the shape
      B does not change after applying both extension operations)

        • Rightwards MVS Extension: let SR be the rightmost MVS separating UB and
           S(Vσ );

        • Rightwards Rainbow Extension: let Rv(UB ) be the vertices of rainbow compo-
           nents incident to UB , and let SR be the set of vertices reachable from S(Vσ )
           without passing through any other vertex in Rv(UB ).

        • Update B: Update E( B) ⊆ E(σ ) to be the subset of edges reachable from
           S(Vσ ) without passing through any vertex in SR . Update UB := SR .

We are now ready to deﬁne the kernel-separator W and shape σA , σB alongside their
color-partitions in the kernel-decomposition as the following,

  1. Let E(σB ) = E( B) be the set of edges that can be reached from S(Vσ ) without
     passing through any vertex in SW , and let E(σA ) = E(σ) \ E( B);

  2. Update TW : Initialize TW := ∅. Add a vertex v from T (Vσ ) \ SW into TW




                                          389
           • if it is a vertex reachable from Uσ without using any edge in E( B); a

           • it is a vertex not connected to Uσ ∪ S(Vσ ) but incident to edges. (In other
                 words, the vertex is part of an edge-component around T (Vσ )).

     3. Call W := SW ∪ TW the kernel-separator for σ. And note that SW ∪ TW is also
        the kernel-partition for W.

     4. Completing their boundary deﬁnition as the following: let UσA := Uσ , VσA =
        UσB := W = SW ∪ TW (with kernel partition given by SW ∪ TW ), and VσB = Vσ . b
     a crucially, this shall be contrasted to vertices reachable without passing through S . See diagram.
                                                                                          W
     b Shape B and σ have the same set of edges and right-boundary, except that the left-boundary U
                        B                                                                               σB
  contains vertices from UB = SW and extra vertices in TW ⊆ UσB ∩ T (VB ) that are held dormant in B.

   With the above procedure for decomposition, we make the following observations.

Observation 8.8.20. Both SW and TW may be empty. Moreover, W may be empty.

Observation 8.8.21 (Vertex’s kernel partition status may ﬂip). It is possible for a vertex to
start from v ∈ T (Vσ ) and becomes an S-vertex v ∈ SW in the decomposition, however TW remains
a subset of T.

Observation 8.8.22 (Vertices in TW are dormant in σB ). TW ⊆ UσB ∩ VσB is not incident to
any edge in σB .

Claim 8.8.23 (Vertices in TW are dormant in σB ). TW ⊆ T (Vσ ) . Moreover, TW ⊆ UσB ∩ VσB
is not incident to any edge in σB .

Proof. TW ⊆ T (Vσ ) is immediate as each vertex is added to TW from T (Vσ ) \ SW . Recall
that any edge in σB is an edge that can be reached from S(Vσ ) without passing through
SW , hence this is also immediate for a vertex added to TW as a vertex not connected to
Uσ ∪ S(Vσ ) but incident to edges. Finally, since SW is a separator for Uσ and S(Vσ ), any
vertex reachable from Uσ without using any edge in E( B) is in SW . However, a vertex may
only get added from T (Vσ ) \ SW . This proves the claim.

                                                   390
Proposition 8.8.24 (Self-cancellation property for trivial TW ). For any right color-shape
(σ, β σ ) = ( A, β A ) ◦ ( B, β B ) with W = VA = SW ∪ TW as its kernel partition, if TW = ∅,
then we have Uσ being the unique MVS separating Uσ and S(Vσ ).

    Moreover, let σ′ be the shape with

   1. E(σ′ ) := E(σ), and identical left-boundary Uσ = Uσ′ ;

   2. The right-boundary is obtained by removing T (Vσ ) vertices, i.e., Vσ′ := Vσ \ T (Vσ );

   3. Remove any deg-0 vertex in V (σ′ ) \ Uσ′ ∪ Vσ′ from V (σ′ ) ;

σ′ continues to be a (color-)right shape in L T .

Proof. We ﬁrst give a proof that highlights the main idea by ignoring the color-dependence,
and then show it continues to hold with color-dependence. In the former case, W = SW
is taken to be the rightmost MVS separating S(Vσ ) and Uσ (with no further rightwards-
extensions). Since Uσ is also separating Uσ and S(Vσ ), we have |Uσ | ≥ |W |.

    We now observe that SW is also a separator for Uσ and T (Vσ ): for any vertex v ∈
T (Vσ ) \ SW , if there is a path from Uσ to v without passing through v, such vertex v is by
construction included into TW , giving us a contradiction. For vertex v ∈ T (Vσ ) ∩ SW , SW
by deﬁnition separates any path from Uσ to v. Therefor, SW is also a separator for Uσ and
T (Vσ ), hence a separator for Uσ and Vσ = S(Uσ ) ∪ T (Vσ ) as well. Since σ is a right-shape,
Uσ is the unique MVS separating Uσ and Vσ , hence we have |W | ≥ |Uσ | with equality only
if W = Uσ . Combining the above yields us W = Uσ . Since W is taken to be the Rightmost
MVS separating Uσ and S(Vσ ), Uσ is also the leftmost separator separating Uσ and S(Vσ ),
we conclude. that Uσ is the unique MVS separating Uσ and S(Vσ ).

    To incorporate the color-dependence, it suﬃces to notice Uσ is also a rainbow-MVS
as σ is a right-shape, and hence Uσ is not incident to any cross-color edge in (σ, CP). To
see the uniqueness, we ﬁrst observe that SW remains as a separator for Uσ and T (Vσ )

                                                    391
by the same argument as above. Let S1 be the original RMVS separating Uσ and S(Vσ )
that ultimately evolves into SW after potentially non-trivial rightwards extensions. Notice
rightwards extension may only unblock path from Uσ to T (Vσ ), hence if SW is a separator
for Uσ and T (Vσ ), S1 is also a separator for Uσ and T (Vσ ). By the same argument earlier,
we can see we have S1 = Uσ , and thus we can deduce there is no non-trivial rightwards
extension, and again Uσ is the unique MVS separating Uσ and S(Vσ ).
    Finally to see σ′ is a right-shape, it suﬃces to verify there is no ﬂoating component in σ′ ,
as otherwise Uσ′ being the unique color MVS and any edge-component being connected
to Vσ′ are suﬃcient condition to guarantee σ′ being a right-shape. And there is no
ﬂoating component in σ′ as component connected to Uσ ∪ S(Uσ ) remains connected to the
left/right boundary in σ′ , while there cannot be edge-component connected to only T (Vσ )
while not to Uσ ∪ S(Uσ ) as any such component’s incidental T vertex is automatically put
in TW , and we have a contradiction here.



Corollary 8.8.25. Any color-shape (σ, β σ ) = ( A, β A ) ◦ ( B, β B ) with kernel-separator TW = ∅
also has SW = U A and A = Id.


Canonical Decomposition of a Kernel Shape                  We now introduce some properties of the
shapes obtained from the decomposition.

Deﬁnition 8.8.26 (Irreducible right shape via decomposition). Call a right color-shape
(σ, β σ ) = ( A, β A ) ◦ ( B, β B ) irreducible if B = Id( B,β B ) from the above process.

Observation 8.8.27. In the simple case of no color-dependence, i.e., each edge is pure-color, σ is
irreducible if S(Vσ ) is an MVS separating Uσ and S(Vσ ).

    We would like to point out that with the above deﬁnition of irreducible right-shapes, Id
is considered to be irreducible. That said, we may further distinguish it with the following
deﬁnition.

                                                     392
Deﬁnition 8.8.28 (Trivial and non-trivial irreducible right-shapes). The Id-shape with Uσ =
Vσ = V (σ) and rainbow( E(Uσ ∩ Vσ )) = ∅ is called trivial. Any other shape is called non-trivial.


Deﬁnition 8.8.29 (Kernel coeﬃcient (right-) shape). For any right-shape σ with kernel partition
on both Uσ = S(Uσ ) ∪ T (Uσ ) and Vσ = S(Vσ ) ∪ T (Vσ ), and T (Uσ ), T (Vσ ) ̸= ∅, , we call it a
kernel-coeﬃcient shape if


     1. It is a right-shape, Uσ is the unique MVS in σ; moreover, S(Uσ ) is the unique MVS
         separating S(Uσ ) and S(Vσ );


     2. It is not identity (the trivial shape);


     3. Any vertex in T (Vσ ) \ Uσ is either a deg-0 vertex, or can be reached from S(Vσ ) without
         passing through Uσ ;


     4. T (Uσ ) ⊆ T (Vσ ).


When a color-partition CP is given, we additionally require there to be no rainbow edge incident to
Uσ .

       We additionally let R be the collection of kernel-coeﬃcient-right-shapes, and deﬁne its matrix
as


 R[(SW , β SW ) ∪ TW , (S, β S ) ∪ T ] :=

             ∑                 ∑          λ R (σ )CP · Mσ,CP [(SW , β SW ) ∪ ( TW , β T ( TW )), (S, β S ) ∪ ( T, β T )]
         (σ,CP)∈R           β T :T →[k]
 kernel-coeﬃcient shape for
    Uσ =(SW ,β SW )∪ TW
       Vσ =(S,β S )∪ T


       Notice that R has indices that does not depend on color of TW and T throughout, while
we sum over the colors of T in its deﬁnition for notational correctness due to the matrix
Mσ,CP are indexed with each vertex receiving some color.

                                                             393
Proposition 8.8.30 (Decomposition of a right shape). For any right (color-)shape (σ, β σ ) =
( A, β A ) ◦ ( B, β B ) decomposed by the above process with color-partition, ( A, β A ) is an irreducible
right-shape (possibly trivial), and ( B, β B ) is either identity (in the case of σ being irreducible) or a
kernel-coeﬃcient right-shape.


Proof. We ﬁrst show A is an irreducible right-shape. By deﬁnition of an irreducible right
shape, we are to show ( A, β A ) = ( A L , β A L ) ◦ ( A R , β AR ) from our kernel-decomposition
process gives A R = Id. That said, consider the track of the rightwards extension process,
and observe that in the base level A = A0 with S A0 being the rightmost MVS for U A and
S(VA ). The subsequent rightwards extensions move S A0 to the right so that we arrive
at S A = VA at the end of the process, and consider the decomposition according to the
iterations
                         A = A0 ◦ X1 ◦ A1 ◦ X2 ◦ · · · ◦ At ◦ Xt ◦( A \ A L )
                             |              {z                 }
                                                AL

for Ai , Xi rightwards-extension-shapes. We then observe that applying decomposition
process on σ undergoes the same iterative process up until obtaining A L , as we are
applying the same process with the same starting point of A0 (rightmost MVS for U A
and S(VA ) remains invariant under composition with a kernel-coeﬃcient shape). Since
the process does not converge until S A = VA , the same extension process for A does not
converge until we have VA L = VA , giving us A R = A \ A L being identity as desired.

    To see B being either an identity or kernel-coeﬃcient-right-shape, it suﬃces to verify
the conditions that B is a right-color-shape, and any vertex in T (VB ) \ T (UB ) is a degree-0
vertex or a vertex reachable from S(VB ) in B without passing through UB .

    We start by verifying any vertex in T (VB ) \ UB is either a deg-0 vertex from T (VB ) \ UB ,
or a vertex reachable from S(Vσ ) without passing through UB . Notice if the vertex is not
connected to S(Vσ ) ∪ UB , yet not degree-0, such vertex is put into TA and added to UB .
Therefore, it suﬃces for us to consider the vertex connected to S(Vσ ) ∪ UB . If the vertex

                                                     394
cannot be reached from S(Vσ ) without passing through UB yet connected to S(Vσ ), there
must be a path from from UB to the vertex, moreover, the path does not use any edge
reachable from S(Vσ ) without passing through SW . However, we have a contradiction here
as any such T vertex is added to TA , and this proves the desired connectivity condition.

   Finally we show B is a color-right-shape. Recall that UB is obtained from adding
T (VB ) \ S A as T vertices to S A . By construction, S A is the unique MVS separating in E( B)
S A and S(Vσ ), therefore it is also the unique MVS separating S A and S(Vσ ) ∪ TS where
TS ⊆ T (Vσ ) is the set of vertices reachable from S(Vσ ) in B without passing through S A .
Since any added vertex to both boundaries is in the mandatory separator, S A ∪ TA is also a
unique MVS for S(Vσ ) ∪ TS ∪ TA . Finally, observe that TS ∪ TA = T (Vσ ) as any vertex from
T (Vσ ) not added to TA is either a degree-0 vertex in T (Vσ ), or a vertex that can be reached
from S(Vσ ) without passing through SB (in which case added via TA ). Moreover, note
that adding TI a set of deg-0 vertex to S(Vσ ) ∪ TS ∪ TA ∪ TI preserves S A ∪ TA = UB being
a unique MVS for both sides, and note that the right boundary is exactly S(Vσ ) ∪ T (Vσ )
now. Therefore, we have shown B is a right-shape. (It is immediate that it also satisﬁes
color-constraints and hence a right-color-shape)

(σ, CP) in R, we deﬁne it as

Remark 8.8.36. For intuition, the reader should think of Irreducible Terms as the desired kernel
for the corresponding kernel-partition. It contains the desired all-1 component, but also contains
extra terms that are necessary.




Set-up     We ﬁrst point out that we are considering vector equality here, and each term
may be viewed as obtained from starting with a ribbon R with ﬁxed right-boundary, i.e.,
VR = (S ∪ T, β S ∪ β T ) or VR = (S, β S ) in the case of L T 1S,β S and remove its right boundary
VR . Therefore, it is equivalent for the purpose of the vector equality that we transform the
ribbon with original right boundary VR = (S, β S ) into a ribbon with right boundary of
VR = (S ∪ T, β S ∪ β T ), and this allows us to focus solely on ribbons with the same right
boundary.

    To handle this discrepancy, for any ribbon R with VR = S, consider the set of vertices
TI ⊆ T (Vσ ) = T such that TI are not incident to any edge in E( R), add TI as deg-0
vertex into V ( R), and ﬁnally modify the right boundary from (S, β S ) to (S ∪ T, β S ∪ β T ).
Formally we have


λ L ( R) β R MR,β( R) [(UR , βUR ), (S, β S )] = ∑ λ L ( R ∪ TI ) · MR∪TI ,β( R)∪ β(TI ) [(UR , βUR ), (S ∪ T, β S ∪ β T )]
                                               β( TI )


where we remind the reader that adding deg-0 vertex outside the left/right boundary

                                                         399
does not aﬀect the graph matrix for a ribbon, i.e.


                                   MR,β( R) = MR∪TI ,β( R)∪ β(TI )


   With this transformation, it remains for us to show a bĳection among ribbons with right
boundary being (S ∪ T, β S ∪ β T ) in the above equality. We prove this by strong induction
by inducting on |S ∪ T |. At this point, it is convenient for us to ignore the labeling of
vertices in [n] and consider shapes, except noting the right boundary have labels given by
S ∪ T.


Partitioning the RHS by W        Recall our desired equality of

    1                             1
  ( √ )|T | · L T 1(S,β S ),T = ( √ )|T | · L T 1(S,β S ) + Composable Terms + Irreducible Terms .
     k                             k

   Viewing the RHS as shapes with right boundary being (S ∪ T, β S ∪ β T ), we note that
the three terms in the equation can be viewed as partitioning based on the intermediate
separator W = SW ∪ TW by the right-shape-decomposition process as the following,


   1. L T 1S,β S corresponds to right shapes with intermediate separators W such that TW =
      ∅;


   2. For right shapes with TW ̸= ∅, we further case on whether W = S ∪ T, if so, this is
      a irreducible shape;


   3. Otherwise, we have W ̸= S ∪ T with TW ̸= ∅, and this is a shape in the composable
      term.


And at the same time, the LHS can be viewed as right-shapes from L T with right boundary
being (S ∪ T, β S ∪ β T ).

                                                400
     The bĳection restricted to the ﬁrst two terms can be proven directly as the following,
and we prove the bĳection for composable term via an induction on |S ∪ T |.


Bĳection for Shapes with TW = ∅ and Intermediate Separator W = S ∪ T

Claim 8.8.37. There is a bĳection between shapes on the LHS with TW = ∅, and shapes in
L T 1S,β S .


Proof. The injective map from the LHS to the RHS follows by the self-cancellation property
in proposition 8.8.24. The other direction follows by observing that for each right-shape
with Vσ = S, let σ′ be the shaped obtained by adding vertices from T to Vσ′ preserves the
shape being a right-shape: as any separator with added vertices into Vσ is also a separator
for the original right-shape. Finally, notice that applying the decomposition process on
σ′ yields an intermediate separator W with TW = ∅, since Uσ′ = SW is also the unique
MVS for Uσ′ and S(Vσ′ ) = Vσ ), and each vertex in T (Vσ′ ) is either reachable from S(Vσ )
without passing Uσ since it appears as a vertex in V (σ ) \ (Uσ ∪ Vσ ), or it is a deg-0 vertex
outside the boundary in σ (i.e. it is a vertex that gets added), and such vertex is not in
TW by our decomposition (factored out by the kernel-coeﬃcient shape). Hence, we have
shown a bĳection between TW = ∅ and L T 1S,β S .




Claim 8.8.38. There is also a bĳection between ribbons on the LHS with W = S ∪ T and (non-
intersected) shapes in the irreducible terms.


Proof. The bĳection on shapes with W = S ∪ T follows by deﬁnition of irreducible shape
with B = Id in the decomposition.


Bĳection for W ̸= S ∪ T with TW ̸= ∅ and Composable Terms

                                                401
Lemma 8.8.39. There is a bĳection between shapes in the composable terms on the RHS, and
shapes with intermediate kernel separator W ̸= S ∪ T with TW ̸= ∅ on the LHS.

Proof. We prove this by induction on |S ∪ T |. The base case is when |S ∪ T | = 1, in
particular, S = ∅, and the hypothesis holds trivially as

  1. Any right shape with Vσ = T has the intermediate separator W = T = S ∪ T, and
     any shape of this form is in the irreducible term. In other words, there are no shape
     with W ̸= S ∪ T and TW ̸= ∅.

  2. As for the composable terms, notice there are no kernel-coeﬃcient term with |Vσ | =
     |S ∪ T | = 1 as we require TW ⊆ W ̸= ∅ and |W | < |S ∪ T |, hence there are no
     composable terms either.

For the induction case, we assume the hypothesis appleid on L T f SW ,TW holds for any W
with TW ̸= ∅ and |W | < |S ∪ T |. From LHS to RHS, notice this holds by construction
as for a shape (σ, β) = ( A, β A ) ◦ ( B, β B ) from the kernel-decomposition process with
intermediate separator W = VA ̸= Vσ and TW ̸= ∅ , there is a unique term on the RHS
from the corresponding W where A is obtained from L T f SW ,TW as an irreducible term and
B is a kernel-coeﬃcient term with UB = W as W ̸= Vσ .
   For the other direction, for any irreducible term A and kernel-coeﬃcient-shape B with
A, B additionally consistent on the kernel-partition on W, observe trivially that R = A ◦ B
is also a ribbon on the LHS while it remains to verify that R is only obtained from A and B
on the RHS, i.e., applying the decomposition process on A ◦ B gives us the decomposition
via the intermediate separator W with prescribed kernel partition.
   Recall that intermediate separator is built from the rainbow-MVS separating U A and
S(VB ), it suﬃces for us to show SW = SW ′ . In fact, we will show that these two separators
are built from the same base separator SW0 = SW ′ before any rightward extension process.
                                                  0

Let X be the set of vertices in W ′ that can be reached from U   A without passing through


                                            402
SW0 , and Z the vertices in W that can be reached without passing through SW ′ , and let
                                                                                        0

                                       0                                            0

reached from S(VB ) without passing through W. We observe the following. Note that we
have SW0 = C ∪ Y ∪ Z and SW ′ = X ∪ Y ∪ D.
                                 0


    1. | X | ≤ |C | with equality only if X = C: SW ′ = X ∪ Y ∪ D is a unique MVS separating
                                                     0

         SW ′ and S(VB ). At the same time, C ∪ Y ∪ D is also separating SW ′ and S(VB );
                                                                            0


    2. | Z | ≤ | D | with equality only Z = D: SW = C ∪ Y ∪ Z is a unique MVS separating
         SW0 and S(VB ). At the same time, C ∪ Y ∪ D is also separating these two sets;

    3. Unless both of the above hold as equalities, X ∪ Y ∪ Z is a smaller separator sepa-
         rating U A and S(VB ), and we have a contradiction here as SW0 and SW ′ both start as
                                                                                0

         the RMVS separating U A and S(VB ). Therefore, we have shown X = C, Z = D and
         SW0 = SW ′ .
                         0


    4. We then observe that by construction of our rightwards extension process, this
         guarantees SW = SW ′ , and additionally TW = TW ′ .

This completes the proof to our bĳection.




Intersection Patterns Put Back       As foreshadowed in the previous proposition, there are
intersection terms that arise in the construction of L T f as we are essentially applying
graph matrix multiplication again, and intersection beyond the boundary injectivity are
expected. More interestingly, it turns out that our constructed kernel L T f (S,β S ),T will
contain intersection terms as they are truly part of the kernel of Q.
     We now give the full characterization for shapes that appear in our ﬁnal result of
L T f (S,β S ),T .

                                               403
Formal Irreducible Kernel for Q

Deﬁnition 8.8.40 (Intersection Shape, and Intersection Pattern). Given a (possibly improper)
shape α obtained from t-way product of shape τ ◦ γ1 ◦ γ2 · · · ◦ γt , we call α an intersection shape.
For each shape α, we let P(α) denote its corresponding intersection pattern deﬁned s the following.
Intersection pattern P(α) is a graph on V (τ ◦ γ1 ◦ γ2 · · · ◦ γt ) such that for each γi , it speciﬁes how
vertices in V (γi ) \ Uγi intersects with vertices in prior level, i.e. vertices in V (τ ◦ γ1 ◦ · · · ◦ γi−1 )
.

Remark 8.8.41. Any proper shape α is also trivially an intersection shape.

Deﬁnition 8.8.42 (Valid Intersection Proﬁle for Kernel). For each intersection pattern Pα with
intersection shape α = τ ◦ γ1 ◦ · · · ◦ γt . We call it a valid intersection proﬁle if each γi is a
right-intersection shape such that ,

    1. S(Uγi ) is the unique color-MVS separating S(Uγi ) and S(Vγi ) for γi ;

    2. and S(Vγi ) is a color-MVS for S(Uγ ) ∪ Int(γi ) and S(Vγi ) where Int(γi ) is the set of
       vertices in γi that intersect with vertices in previous level beyond the necessary boundary in
       Uγi ;

    3. T (Uγi ) ⊆ T (Vγi ), and any vertex in T (Uγi ) is not incident to any edge; any vertex in
       T (Vγi ) \ T (Uγi ) is reachable from S(Vγi ) without passing through S(Uγi ).

    4. In other words, γi is a kernel-coeﬃcient shape in R for (S(Uγi ), β S(Uγ ) ) ∪ T (Uγi ) and
                                                                                         i

       (S(Vγi ), β S(Vγ ) ) ∪ T (Vγi )
                       i



and additionally,

    1. τ is a proper irreducible right-shape, i.e., S(Vτ ) is the rightmost color-MVS separating
       Uτ = Uα and S(Vτ ).

Lemma 8.8.43. Deﬁne

to RHS, in particular, the cancellation of intersections that does not satisfy the condition

restricted to σ be Pσ . In the case σ is not a proper-intersection right-shape under Pσ , i.e.,
S(Vσ ) is not the color-MVS for S(Uσ ) ∪ Int(σ ), we claim that we can another term of the

                                                                 405
same shape on the LHS (that comes with a ﬂipped sign) so that they cancel and do not
contribute to the RHS of L T f (S,β S ),T . To see this, let γ be the proper-intersection shape
on (σ, Pσ ), and note that we can write σ = γ ◦ (σ − γ) where we take Vγ to be the color-
MVS for σ under intersection pattern Pσ . By induction hypothesis, the shape α ◦ γ with
intersection proﬁle P(α) ∪ Pγ where we let Pγ be the intersection pattern of Pσ restricted
to the γ part appears in L T f S(Uγ ),T (Uγ ) with coeﬃcient (−1)| Pα |+1 λ L (α ◦ γ) where we note
that the additional +1 comes from the outmost intersection level of γ. Therefore, these
two shapes cancel each other and do not contribute the ﬁnal RHS.

   On the other hand, if σ is a proper intersection-right-shape, such term appears on the
RHS with an extra factor of (−1) from the minus sign ahead of the composable terms. And
importantly, observe that such shape with the prescribed intersection pattern continues
to admit the characterization of a valid intersection proﬁle for kernel term.

      The analysis in this section is largely identical to the analysis in the fashion of section 8.6
and section 8.7 with minor modiﬁcation adjusted to the structural properties. We start by
applying piecewise decomposition of each irreducible right shape,

                                                       408
8.9.1 Piecewise Decomposition of Irreducible Right Shape

A color-right-shape (σ, CPσ ) is called irreducible if its kernel-decomposition (σ, CPσ ) =
( A, CP A ) ◦ ( B, CPB ) via our prescribed process gives B = Id. That said, by tracking
our recursive process that yields the decomposition of A ◦ B, we also obtain a ﬁner
decomposition of A as the following. Recall that the process keeps track of intermediate
separator SWt throughout, even though the process does not append TWt vertices for the
intermediate level, observe that we may apply the TW vertices for intermediate separators,
and obtain Wt = SWt ∪ TWt .

    Formally, for each (rightwards) extension, we factor out edges and vertices involved,
and deﬁne the extension piece as

Deﬁnition 8.9.2. A kernel (rightwards) extension piece γ from adjacent separators SWt and SWt+1
is a shape deﬁned as the following. Assume each intermediate separators comes with additional TWt
vertices,

   1. Let Uγ = SWt ∪ TWt , and Vγ = SWt+1 ∪ TWt+1 with inherited kernel partition. In other
      words, S(Uγ ) = SWt , and T (Vγ ) = TWt . and similarly for Vγ .

   2. Let E(γ) be the set of edges that can be reached from S(Vγ ) without passing through any
      vertex in Uγ .

Deﬁnition 8.9.3 (Kernel rightwrds-extension shape: rainbow). A shape ( X, CPX ) with kernel
partitions on both boundaries is a kernel rightwrads-MVS-extension shape if

   1. UX is a unique MVS for X ;

   2. Any vertex in S(VX ) is in the same rainbow component as S(UX ): each vertex in S(VX ) is
      in the rainbow component of some vertex in S(UX ) under CPX .

   3. T (UX ) ⊆ T (VX ) and any vertex in T (UX ) ∩ T (VX ) is dormant in X.

                                               409
   4. Any vertex in T (VX ) \ T (UX ) is reachable from S(VX ) without passing through UX .

Additionally there are kernel-partitions for both UX and VX , and any vertex in T (UX ) is dormant
in X, i.e., it is in T (UX ) ∩ T (VX ) and degree-0.

Remark 8.9.4. Notice we may have an T vertex in T (Vσ ) \ T (Uσ ) joining the boundary yet it is
connected via a pure-color edge.

Deﬁnition 8.9.5 (Kernel rightwrds-extension shape: MVS). A shape (Y, CPY ) with kernel
partition on both boundaries is a kernel rightwrads-MVS-extension shape if

   1. S(Vγ ) is the unique MVS separating S(Uγ ) and S(Vγ ), moreover, VY is a unique MVS for
        Y.

   2. Any edge in E(Y ) incident to UY is pure-color under CP.

   3. T (UX ) ⊆ T (VX ) and any vertex in T (UX ) ∩ T (VX ) is dormant in X.

   4. Any vertex in T (VX ) \ T (UX ) is reachable from S(VX ) without passing through UX .

Let τeP be the intersection result in τ ◦ Y.

Proposition 8.9.6. For an irreducible color-right-shape ( A, CP A ), we have


         ( A, CP A ) = (τ, CPτ ) ◦ ( X1 , CPX1 ) ◦ (Y1 , CPY1 ) ◦ · · · ◦ ( Xt , CPXt ) ◦ (Yt , CPYt )


where

   1. τ is a base piece such that S(Vτ ) is an MVS separating Uτ and S(Vτ );

   2. ( Xi , CPXi ) for i ∈ [t − 1] is a kernel-rightwards-rainbow-extension shape;

   3. (Yi , CPYi ) for i ∈ [t − 1] is a kernel-rightwards-MVS-extension shape;

                                                     410
With some abuse of notation, we allow the ﬁnal extension Yt to be trivial (i.e. identity).

Proof. Notice may view the base piece τ as an MVS-extension, thus it suﬃces for us to
show each γ piece factored out is an MVS/rainbow-extension piece satisfy the desired
properties.

    Let γ be a rightwards rainbow-extension from St to St+1 (along with Tt and Tt+1 their
corresponding T vertices). By construction of rainbow extension from St to St+1 , any
vertex in St+1 is the boundary of rainbow components incident to St that can be reached
from VA , and hence S(Vγ ) = St+1 are in the same rainbow components as S(Uγ ) = St .

    For the other case, let γ be a rightwards MVS-extension St to St+1 (along with Tt and Tt+1
their corresponding T vertices). The condition S(Vγ ) being the unique MVS separating
S(Uγ ) and S(Vγ ) follows immediately by deﬁnition from MVS-extension. To see that Uγ is
a unique MVS, notice Uγ = St ∪ Tt and as we show below, Tt ⊆ T (Uγ ) ∩ T (Vγ ) is dormant
in γ, therefore Uγ is the MVS separating Uγ and Vγ ∪ T (Uγ ). Finally adding vertices in
T (Vγ ) \ T (Uγ ) preserves the MVS and proves the claim.

    To verify T (UX ) ⊆ T (VX ), note that any vertex is added to T (UX ) either has a path to
U A in X without using edges to the right of St (i.e. edges reachable from S(VA ) without
passing through S A ), or a T vertex not connected to U A ∪ S(VA ) yet incident to edges. In
the former case, any such path is a also a path from U A without using edges to the right of
St+1 , hence such vertex continues to be in Tt+1 . The latter case is immediate as any such
component is in TWt regardless of the rightwards-extensions. Additionally, observe that
any vertex in T (Uγ ) ∩ T (Vγ ) is dormant in γ as any T vertex in Uγ is not incident to edges
to the right of St = S(Uγ ) while any edge in γ is to the right of St by deﬁniton.

This completes our bound for ∥MK (σ),CP ∥ by the identical argument in the norm bound
for color-shape.


   With the above weight function, we apply the same idea of factorizing the weight
function into pieces.

   To show this, we ﬁrst apply process each component in τ, and then remove possible

      on the RHS;
